<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> Which modification is annotated at the alanine at position 2?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> N-formylalanine<br><br><strong>B.</strong> N-acetylalanine<br><br><strong>C.</strong> N-methylalanine<br><br><strong>D.</strong> N-succinylalanine<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: Looking at the protein sequence, position 1 is Methionine (M) and position 2 is Alanine (A). In eukaryotic proteins, the initiator methionine (Met1) is frequently cleaved co-translationally, and the newly exposed N-terminal residue often undergoes post-translational modification. N-terminal acetylation is the most common protein modification in eukaryotes, occurring on approximately 80-90% of cytosolic proteins. Alanine is one of the most commonly acetylated residues after Met cleavage. <br><br>Considering the options:<br>- N-formylalanine is rare and typically associated with prokaryotic or mitochondrial proteins<br>- N-acetylalanine is the most prevalent N-terminal modification in eukaryotic proteins, especially at position 2 after Met1 cleavage<br>- N-methylalanine is not a common N-terminal modification<br>- N-succinylalanine is primarily observed as a lysine modification in mitochondrial proteins<br><br>The biological context strongly supports N-acetylalanine as the most appropriate modification at position 2.<br><br>2. answer: B</td>
<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> Using one-based residue numbering, which N-terminal chemical modification is annotated for the alanine at position 2?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> N-formylalanine<br><br><strong>B.</strong> N-acetylalanine<br><br><strong>C.</strong> N-methylalanine<br><br><strong>D.</strong> N-succinylalanine<br><br><strong>Skills:</strong> active_site_0000:1<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>## Analysis<br><br>**Protein Sequence (first few residues):**<br>- Position 1: **M** (Methionine) — initiator methionine<br>- Position 2: **A** (Alanine)<br><br>**Reasoning:**<br><br>1. **Initiator Met cleavage:** In many eukaryotic proteins, the N-terminal methionine (position 1) is co- or post-translationally cleaved by methionine aminopeptidases (MetAPs).<br><br>2. **New N-terminal residue:** After Met removal, the alanine at position 2 becomes the new N-terminus of the protein.<br><br>3. **N-terminal modification:** Once the new N-terminal residue is exposed, it can undergo chemical modification. N-terminal acetylation is the **most prevalent** N-terminal modification in eukaryotic proteins, occurring on ~80-90% of human proteins. This reaction is catalyzed by N-terminal acetyltransferases (NATs).<br><br>4. **Alanine preference:** Alanine is one of the residues most commonly acetylated at the N-terminus after Met cleavage (along with serine, threonine, and glycine). The small size of alanine makes it an ideal substrate for NAT enzymes.<br><br>5. **Evaluating options:**<br>   - **A (N-formylalanine):** More characteristic of prokaryotic systems.<br>   - **B (N-acetylalanine):** Most common N-terminal modification in eukaryotic proteins for alanine.<br>   - **C (N-methylalanine):** Rare modification, not typical for N-terminal alanine.<br>   - **D (N-succinylalanine):** Uncommon, not a standard N-terminal modification.<br><br>The most biologically appropriate annotation for alanine at position 2 as a new N-terminal residue is **N-acetylation**.<br><br>## Answer<br><br>1. reasoning: After removal of the initiator methionine, alanine at position 2 becomes the new N-terminal residue. N-terminal acetylation is the most common N-terminal modification in eukaryotic proteins, and alanine is a preferred substrate for N-terminal acetyltransferases (NATs).<br>2. answer: B</td>
